Jesus E. Vazquez
1∗

Abstract




Establishing the Parallels and Differences Between Right-Censored and Missing Covariates

Abstract

While right-censored time-to-event outcomes have been studied for decades, handling time-to-event covariates, also known as right-censored covariates, is now of growing interest. So far, the literature has treated right-censored covariates as distinct from missing covariates, overlooking the potential applicability of estimators to both scenarios. We bridge this gap by establishing connections between right-censored and missing covariates under various assumptions about censoring and missingness, allowing us to identify parallels and differences to determine when estimators can be used in both contexts. These connections reveal adaptations to five estimators for right-censored covariates in the unexplored area of informative covariate right-censoring and to formulate a new estimator for this setting, where the event time depends on the censoring time. We establish the asymptotic properties of the six estimators, evaluate their robustness under incorrect distributional assumptions, and establish their comparative efficiency. We conducted a simulation study to confirm our theoretical results, and then applied all estimators to a Huntington disease observational study to analyze cognitive impairments as a function of time to clinical diagnosis.

Some Key Words
:
censored covariate; missing data; not at random; informative;
model misspecification; robustness; statistical efficiency

Short title
: Parallels between right-censored covariates and missing covariates

1

Introduction

Efforts are now underway to design clinical trials for Huntington disease aimed at slowing or stopping the disease before irreversible damage occurs
(
Tabrizi et al., 2022b
)
. A key aspect of that design is modeling how disease symptoms worsen over time—the symptom trajectory—in the years leading up to a diagnosis because with knowledge of that symptom trajectory, researchers can identify when and how to test experimental therapies. Yet modeling the symptom trajectory is not easy because Huntington disease
progresses slowly over decades, so studies that track symptoms often end before a diagnosis can be made. This makes time to diagnosis
right-censored
(i.e., a patient will reach the criteria for a diagnosis sometime after , or “to the right of”, the last study visit, but exactly when is unknown), leaving researchers with the challenge of trying to model the symptom trajectory without full information about when a diagnosis occurs.

This challenge creates a unique statistical problem of modeling the symptom trajectory as a function of a
right-censored covariate
, time to diagnosis. Tackling this problem by adopting estimators from the missing data literature has long been the go-to strategy
(
Lotspeich et al., 2024a
)
, as the true covariate value—whether right-censored or missing—is sometimes observed and sometimes not. Right-censoring and missingness are well known to be different, yet many estimators in the right-censored covariate literature seem to share similar statistical properties as estimators used in the missing covariate literature
(
Bartlett et al., 2014a
;
Bernhardt et al., 2015a
;
Ahn et al., 2018a
;
Atem et al., 2019a
;
Matsouaka & Atem, 2020a
)
. These similarities blur the distinctions between the right-censored covariate problem and missing covariate problem. When we blindly apply estimators designed for one problem to the other, sometimes they behave similarly, and at other times they do not, even though the assumptions made in both problems are similar.

That flip-flopping, where the estimators sometimes behave similarly and other times do not, led us to ask: How do the subtle differences between right censoring and missingness affect the estimators and their statistical properties? Neglecting this question leaves us vulnerable to using the incorrect estimator due to an unacknowledged blind spot. Not using the correct estimator may result in bias and inefficiency in estimating the parameters indexing symptom trajectory models, posing two major challenges when striving to design a well-powered clinical trial that tests an experimental therapy.

We tackle this blind spot head-on. We start by outlining the right-censored and missing covariate problems, highlighting the parallels and distinctions between different types of covariate missingness (i.e., missing at random, missing not at random) and covariate right-censoring (i.e., noninformative, informative) (Sections
2
and
4
). These insights help us identify when estimators for one problem can be applied to the other, when they cannot, and the driving reasons behind these differences (Sections
3
and
4
). We provide practical guidance on implementing the estimators, complete with R software and documentation for reproducibility (Section
5
). We provide the first-ever theoretical and empirical justifications for when each estimator remains consistent despite distribution misspecifications and how they compare in statistical efficiency (Sections
6
and
7
). By applying the estimators to Huntington disease data
we show how the estimators perform in practice and how their different properties impact conclusions made about the progression patterns of cognitive symptom trajectories in the years leading up to a diagnosis (Section
8
). All these findings are distilled into practical guidance (Section
9
), empowering researchers to choose the right estimator without making blind assumptions.

2

Framework for right-censoring and missingness

2.1

Notation and assumptions

We consider the regression model

\displaystyle{Y}=m(X,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})+{\epsilon},\ \ \ \epsilon\sim\hbox{Normal}(0,\sigma^{2}),

(1)

which models the relationship between an outcome
Y
and covariates
X
and
{\mathbf{Z}}
. That relationship is determined by a mean function
m(\cdot)
, which is assumed to be known up to a parameter
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
that is of length
p
. We assume throughout that the values for
{\mathbf{Z}}
are always observed, but those for
X
are not.

We consider two distinct problems in which
X
is not always observed. The first, known as the right-censored covariate problem, is when
X
is right-censored. Instead of observing
X
, we observe
W=\min(X,C)
and
\Delta=I(X\leq C)
, where
C
is a random censoring variable. In our Huntington disease problem,
W
represents the minimum value between the time to diagnosis
X
and the random time to study exit
C
, and
\Delta
is the censoring indicator that is 1 if
X
is observed and 0 if not. The second, known as the missing covariate problem, is when
X
is missing. In this problem, we observe a missingness indicator
R
which is 1 if
X
is observed and 0 if
X
is missing. The observed data when
X
is right-censored are
{\mathbf{O}}^{\rm cens}=(Y,W,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}})
, and when
X
is missing are
{\mathbf{O}}^{\rm miss}=(Y,RX,R,{\mathbf{Z}})
. The first goal is to derive consistent estimators for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\equiv({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{{}^{\rm T}},\sigma)^{{}^{\rm T}}
in both problems, and the second goal is to highlight how those estimators differ across the two problems.

Toward these goals, we first consider simple, yet common assumptions made in the statistical literature about the right-censored and missing covariate problems. Let
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}
represent statistical independence. When
X
is potentially right-censored, we assume that
C
and
Y
are independent given
X
and
{\mathbf{Z}}
(i.e.,
C\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}
) and that
X
and
C
are independent given
{\mathbf{Z}}
(i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
). The last assumption is known as noninformative covariate censoring and occurs when the event rate (e.g, clinical diagnosis) is the same for individuals who were observed or censored conditional on the set of fully observed covariates
(
Lee & Wolfe, 1998a
)
. For example, overall, the event rate may not be the same among those who were observed or censored, but equal event rates are obtained conditional on ethnicity (i.e.,
{\mathbf{Z}}
).

We will make assumptions in the missing covariate problem that parallel those used in the right-censored covariate problem so that we may pinpoint if and how the two problems differ. The analog to these assumptions when
X
is missing are:
R
and
Y
are independent given
X
and
{\mathbf{Z}}
(i.e.,
R\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}
), and
X
and
R
are independent given
{\mathbf{Z}}
(i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}R|{\mathbf{Z}}
). The latter assumption is known as the missing at random assumption, implying that the missingness in
X
is independent of
X
given the observed data
(
Little & Rubin, 2019a
)
. The noninformative covariate right-censoring and missing at random assumptions are analogous in that they assume
X
is independent of
C
or
R
. For that reason, it will help to refer to these assumptions as independent censoring and independent missingness because, in both cases, we assume conditional independence.

Throughout, we will use superscripts “cens” and “miss” to differentiate between right-censoring and missingness, respectively. We will also use “ind” to denote independence, which will refer to independent censoring (i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the right-censored covariate problem and to independent missingness (i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}R|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the missing covariate problem. To minimize notation we will let
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}(C,R)
indicate independent right-censoring or missingness. We will denote different (conditional) densities
f
with subscripts to differentiate between densities. For example,
f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,w,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
denotes the conditional density function of
Y
given
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
evaluated at
Y=y
,
X=w
, and
{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}}
. Similarly,
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\cdot)
will be used to denote the conditional expectation with respect to the density
f_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. For a vector or matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
, we define
{\mathbf{M}}^{\otimes 2}\equiv{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm T}
.

2.2

The distinction between right-censoring and missingness

At first glance, a right-censored covariate seems no different from a missing covariate since, in both cases, we do not always observe the true covariate value. Yet, right-censoring is different from missingness in one major way. With missingness, we have no
information about the true covariate value. With right-censoring, we have partial information: if an individual had been in a study for five years and the study ended before a diagnosis was made, we know their time to diagnosis is at least five years.
That partial information, though seemingly small, impacts how we handle the unobserved
X
. One way we see that impact is when computing the probability that
X
is observed. That probability changes depending on whether we are handling a right-censored or missing covariate. When
X
is subject to right-censoring, the probability
X
is observed is

\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(x,{\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\int_{x<c}f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,{\mathbf{z}})dc.

When
X
is subject to missingness, the probability that
X
is observed is

\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}(x,{\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(R=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\hbox{pr}(R=1|{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}).

Justification of these calculations is given in Sections
S.2
and
S.3
. Comparing the above, we see that in the missing covariate problem, the probability that
X
is observed is only a function of
{\mathbf{Z}}
. In the right-censored covariate problem, however, that same probability is
not
just a function of
{\mathbf{Z}}
. In that problem, having
X
observed means
C
is larger than
X
, and that information is reflected by the domain of the integral bound (i.e.,
x<c
).
If we were to blindly use the probability from the missing covariate problem in the right-censored covariate problem, we would incorrectly assume that we could replace
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
with
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
. Yet, in the right-censored covariate problem, the probability
X
is observed is
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
, which is not equal to
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{z}})
. These differences affect the estimators used for covariate right-censoring and missingness, as shown next.

3

Comparison of estimators for right-censoring and missingness

The estimators we construct are solutions to so-called
m
-estimating equations
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm est}({\mathbf{O}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\mathbf{0}}
, where the estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
solves these equations
(
Tsiatis, 2006a
)
. We show in Table
1
when and how these estimators differ between the right-censored and missing covariate problems.

3.1

Complete case (CC) estimator

The simplest way to estimate the parameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
when
X
is right-censored or missing is using the CC estimator. This estimator discards all observations where some data are unobserved—right-censored or missing—and estimates the parameters with the remaining data. The estimator for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
based on only the complete cases is the solution to the estimating equations of the CC estimator in Table
1
. Apart from minor notational differences—using
\Delta
or
R
to denote when
X
is observed—these estimating equations are the same for the right-censored covariate problem and the missing covariate problem. Thus, the CC estimator requires no adjustment when adapting it from the missing data literature.

3.2

Inverse probability weighting (IPW) estimator

The IPW estimator is a modification of the CC estimator. It assigns weights to the data to reflect what the full data sample would be if all data were observed, then estimates the parameters. The weights used are the inverse of the probability that
X
is observed and that probability depends on whether we have a right-censored covariate or a missing covariate. In the right-censored covariate problem, the weights are
1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
, whereas in the missing covariate problem, the weights are
1/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})
(Table
1
; IPW estimator).

Table 1
:
Estimating equations used in the right-censored covariate problem when censoring is noninformative (i.e., independent censoring) and in the missing covariate problem when missingness is at random (i.e., independent missingness).

Complete case (CC) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})={\mathbf{0}}

Inverse probability weighting (IPW) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}_{i})={\mathbf{0}}

Maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MLE}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggr[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+(1-\delta_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T}}\hbox{log}\biggr\{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y_{i},x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i})dx\biggr\}\biggr]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MLE}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggr[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+(1-r_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T}}\hbox{log}\biggr\{\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y_{i},x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i})dx\biggr\}\biggr]={\mathbf{0}}

Augmented complete case (ACC) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm ACC}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{\delta_{i}-\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}\}\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm ACC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{r_{i}-\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Modified augmented complete case (MACC) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MACC}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-\delta_{i}/\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}\}\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MACC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-r_{i}/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm AIPW}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}+\{1-\delta_{i}/\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})$}}\}\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$}}\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm AIPW}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-r_{i}/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Note: cens: right-censoring; miss: missingness; ind: independent censoring (i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the right-censored covariate problem and independent missingness (i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}R|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the missing covariate problem. All estimating equations use the score function
{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}})=\partial\hbox{log}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})/\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. Differences from the missing covariate estimating equations are highlighted in
\textstyle\rm gray
. Specific terms are defined below.

\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\int_{x<c}f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,{\mathbf{z}})dc
,

\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
;

\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
,

\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}},\Delta=1}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(X,{\mathbf{z}})-1\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}
.

3.3

Maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)

The MLE maximizes the likelihood of the data to estimate the parameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. The resulting estimator is the solution to the MLE estimating equations shown in Table
1
. These equations contain two parts: the first is based on the likelihood of the data when
X
is observed and the second is based on the likelihood of the data when
X
is not observed. That second part involves integrating over the possible range of values for
X
, and that range differs for the right-censored covariate problem and for the missing covariate problem. When
X
is right-censored, we have the partial information that
C<X
, so the integration is computed over this range. When
X
is missing, we do not have similar partial information, so the integral is computed over all possible values for
X
. These differences mean that the estimating equations for the MLE cannot be interchanged between the right-censored covariate problem and the missing covariate problem.

3.4

Augmented estimators

A challenge with the CC and IPW estimators is their inefficiency, as they discard any observations where some data are unobserved. Augmented estimators address this challenge by creating augmented versions of the CC and IPW estimators to increase their efficiency. The augmented versions are created by comparing the variance of the “original” estimators (i.e., CC and IPW) with the variance of their augmented version (i.e., the augmented estimator).

The variances of two estimators can be compared by analyzing the variances of their influence functions since there is a one-to-one correspondence between estimators and influence functions
(
Rotnitzky & Robins, 1997a
;
Tsiatis, 2006a
)
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG}
denote the influence functions for an original estimator and the augmented estimator. Suppose that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm cov}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG}-{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}},{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG})=\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG})-{\rm cov}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG},{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}).

(2)

This condition, we call the “efficiency condition,” ensures
that the
augmented estimator is less variable (i.e.,

\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG})<\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})
) since, when it holds, we have

\displaystyle\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})-\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG})=\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG})+\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})-2\hbox{cov}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG},{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})=\hbox{var}({\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\rm AUG}-{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})>0.

When the efficiency condition holds, the augmented estimator will be more efficient than the original estimator. We thus use the efficiency condition to create augmented versions of the CC and IPW estimators; derivations of these augmented estimators can be found in the proof of Theorem
1
(Sections
S.2.5
and
S.3.5
).

3.4.1

Augmented CC (ACC) estimator

The ACC estimator, initially proposed by
Bartlett et al. (2014a)
for
X
missing not at random, augments the CC estimator to increase efficiency. We now adapt the ACC estimator to account for independent covariate right-censoring and missingness. The estimating equations have two parts: the first is the same part used in the CC estimator and the second is the augmented part designed to satisfy the efficiency condition in equation (
2
). This augmented part has two components: the first is a function of the probability that
X
is observed and the second is a vector of length equal to that of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and a function of the complete data (
Y,{\mathbf{Z}}
).

The ACC estimating equations for the right-censored covariate problem and the missing covariate problem look very similar (Table
1
; ACC estimator). Yet, interchanging
(RX,R)
with
(W,\Delta)
in the estimating equation for the missing covariate problem will lead to an inconsistent estimator. For the right-censored covariate problem, consistency and the efficiency condition in equation (
2
) is satisfied when the augmented part uses the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens}\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
and augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens}
, as defined in Table
1
. The probability and augmented component differ from those used in the missing covariate problem, where the probability is
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss}(y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
, which simplifies to
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}
because
R\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
, and the augmented component is
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}
.

We prove in Theorem
1
that the ACC estimator for the right-censored covariate problem produces a consistent estimator only when the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens}
is correctly specified, which is equivalent to requiring the distribution
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
to be correctly specified. Correctly specifying
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
may initially seem like a deterrence, but because the data
(Y,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}})
are always observed, practical strategies exist for estimating
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
accurately; see Section
5
.

3.4.2

Modified ACC (MACC) estimator

The ACC estimator was originally designed based on the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
to take advantage of the fully observed data
(Y,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}})
. Given the additional information obtained in the right-censored covariate problem, an analyst may be interested in using the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. While this choice of probability leads to a consistent estimator, it also requires a different formulation of the ACC estimator, which we call the modified ACC (MACC) estimator. The different formulation of the augmented component is required to ensure higher efficiency than that of the CC estimator when using the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. The MACC estimator may be preferable to the ACC estimator if there is a higher chance of correctly specifying the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
than correctly specifying the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Even if there is a higher chance of specifying
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
correctly, correctly estimating this probability is more challenging since we do not always observe
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
. Still, in Section
5
, we discuss practical strategies for appropriately estimating
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}
.

The albeit more challenging version only complicates the estimation process when
X
is right-censored, not when
X
is missing. When
X
is missing, the ACC and MACC estimators are equivalent. If
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
and
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
are plugged into their respective estimating equations, the estimating equations corresponding to the MACC and the ACC estimators are the same (Table
1
). This result does not hold for the right-censored covariate problem since plugging
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
and
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,ind}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
into their respective estimating equations will lead to different estimating equations.

3.4.3

Augmented IPW (AIPW) estimator

Another augmented estimator typically used in the missing covariate problem is the AIPW estimator, which augments the IPW estimator to increase efficiency
(
Rotnitzky & Robins, 1997a
;
Seaman & Vansteelandt, 2018a
)
. To the best of our knowledge, only one AIPW estimator has been developed for censored covariates; specifically, it is for a survival outcome with a time-varying covariate that is subject to interval-censoring
(
Ahn et al., 2018a
)
. We consider a non-survival outcome and the more general setting in which the covariate is randomly right-censored and not time-dependent.

The AIPW estimator contains two parts in its estimating equations (Table
1
; AIPW estimator): the first part is the part used in the IPW estimator and the second is the augmented part designed to satisfy the efficiency condition in equation (
2
). Similar to the ACC and MACC estimators, the AIPW estimator designed for the missing covariate problem cannot be applied to the right-censored covariate problem. While the estimating equations for the AIPW estimator look similar in both problems, the probabilities
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,ind}
and
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}
are different, as well as their augmented components.

3.4.4

Guaranteeing statistical efficiency and reducing computation cost

The efficiency condition in equation (
2
) was used to derive the augmented estimators—ACC, MACC, and AIPW—and guarantee their improved efficiency. However, that derivation assumed that the probability (i.e.,
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
or
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) and the augmented component (i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
) were correctly specified. If either are incorrectly specified, improved efficiency is not guaranteed. To guarantee the efficiency condition in equation (
2
) even when the probability or the augmented component is misspecified, we update the augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
by pre-multiplying the augmented component (as defined in Table
1
) by a fixed matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
.

We use the efficiency condition in equation (
2
) to derive the form of
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. Instead of solving for the form of
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
that satisfies the efficiency condition, we solve for
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. This
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
is a function of the probability (e.g.,
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) and the
augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
; therefore, for any specification of the probability and
the augmented component, we obtain a corresponding
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
matrix that satisfies the efficiency condition in equation (
2
). This strategy leads to an updated augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
that modifies the estimating equations for the ACC, MACC, and AIPW estimators; see Table
.

Whether we use
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
or not, we still need to calculate the augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
, which is not straightforward since it involves computing at least one expectation (ratio of two expectations for the right-censored covariate problem). We propose using
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm close}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
to define
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm close}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
across all augmented estimators. This choice has a closed form when
X
is linear in
m(\cdot)
and when we assume
X
follows a normal distribution (shown in Section
S.1
). We are allowed to make this choice since we prove it does not affect the consistency of the augmented estimators (Sections
S.2
and
S.3
).

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
of Table

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
, where
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
is as follows:

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}=-E[\{1-R/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]E([\{1-R/\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

, any function of the same length as

. Major differences from the missing covariate estimating equations are highlighted in
