.

4

Adjustment of estimators to handle dependent right-censoring and dependent missingness

4.1

Dependency assumptions

Until now, we have assumed independent covariate right-censoring (i.e.,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
), also known as noninformative covariate right-censoring. This assumption is violated when the event rate differs between individuals whose age at diagnosis is observed and those whose age is right-censored. For instance, in Huntington disease, this scenario could occur if the event rate is higher among individuals whose age is right-censored, who may possess an unknown predisposition (associated with
X
) that is not present among individuals whose age at diagnosis was observed. Ignoring informative covariate right-censoring may result in incorrectly estimating how impairments change as a function of time to diagnosis. Establishing estimators that adjust for informative covariate right-censoring (i.e.,
X\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
) will help avoid this incorrect estimation in Huntington disease studies.

The analog to informative covariate right-censoring in the missing covariate problem is missingness not at random (i.e.,
X\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}R|{\mathbf{Z}}
). In this type of missingness, there is a pattern to the missingness that depends on
X
; in our example, those with the unknown predisposition (associated with
X
) are the ones whose data are more likely to be missing. Whether the covariate censoring is informative or the missingness is not at random, there is dependency between
X
and
(C,R)
that is not explained by the observed data. We will therefore refer to these assumptions as dependent censoring and dependent missingness. The superscript “dep” will be used to denote when a estimator uses these dependency assumptions. Moreover, we clarify that the dependency is with
X

and not with
Y
, meaning that the right-censoring and missingness remains conditionally independent of the symptoms given time at diagnosis and the set of the fully observed covariates, i.e.,
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
.

4.2

Impact of dependency on the distinction between right-censoring and missingness

In both the right-censored and missing covariate problems, an underlying mechanism that depends on
X
explains why
X
is not observed. What distinguishes the right-censored covariate problem from the missing covariate problem is the partial information about
X
(i.e., when
X
is right-censored, its true value is larger than
C
).
Under the dependent censoring assumption, the probability that
X
is observed when it is subject to right-censoring is

\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\int_{x<c}f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,x,{\mathbf{z}})dc.

Under the dependent missingness assumption, the probability that
X
is observed is

\displaystyle\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(R=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}}).

When we assumed independent censoring and missingness, the probability that
X
is known was only a function of
{\mathbf{Z}}
in the missing covariate problem and a function of
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
in the right-censored covariate problem. Now, under dependent censoring and missingness, the probability that
X
is known is a function of
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
in both problems. This change occurs because both problems now need to account for the dependency between
X
and
(C,R)
. Thus, under dependent censoring and missingness problems, the probability
X
is observed is the same. However, the partial information provided by the right-censored covariate problem provides more information, reducing the number of unverifiable assumptions required to estimate these probabilities. In Section
5
, we explain what assumptions are needed to correctly model
f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
f_{R|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, i.e., the distributions needed to estimate these probabilities.

4.3

Impact of dependency on the estimators

4.3.1

CC estimator

The estimating equations for the CC estimator rely only on the distribution
f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, so the CC estimator stays the same whether
X
is right-censored or missing, and whether the covariate right-censoring and missingness is independent or dependent.

4.3.2

IPW estimator

The probability in the IPW estimator changes to
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}
in the right-censored covariate problem and to
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}
in the missing covariate problem (Table
3
).

Table 3
:
Estimating equations used in the right-censored covariate problem when censoring is informative (i.e., dependent censoring) and in the missing covariate problem when missingness is not at random (i.e., dependent missingness).

Inverse probability weighting (IPW) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})={\mathbf{0}}

Maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MLE}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggr[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+(1-\delta_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T}}\hbox{log}\biggr\{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\displaystyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{w_{i}<x}$}}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y_{i},x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})dx\biggr\}\biggr]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MLE}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggr[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+(1-r_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T}}\hbox{log}\biggr\{\int_{{\cal D}(x)}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y_{i},x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|R,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,r_{i}=0,{\mathbf{z}}_{i})dx\biggr\}\biggr]={\mathbf{0}}

Augmented complete case (ACC) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm ACC}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{\delta_{i}-\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm ACC}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{r_{i}-\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,ind}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Modified augmented complete case (MACC) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MACC}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-\delta_{i}/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm MACC}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-r_{i}/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) estimator

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm AIPW}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\delta_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-\delta_{i}/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm AIPW}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}_{i}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[r_{i}{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y_{i},x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})+\{1-r_{i}/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}(y_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\right]={\mathbf{0}}

Note:
dep: dependent censoring (i.e.,
X\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}C|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the right-censored covariate problem and dependent missingness (i.e.,
X\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}R|{\mathbf{Z}}
) for the missing covariate problem; definitions of cens, miss, and
{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}})
are as defined in Table
1
. Major differences from the missing covariate estimating equations are highlighted in
\textstyle\rm gray
. Specific terms are defined below.

\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})=\int_{x<c}f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,x,{\mathbf{z}})dc
,

\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
;

\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
,

\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(R=1|Y=y,{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}})
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}},\Delta=1}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}},R=1}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})\}
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})-1\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})}\}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})-1\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}}
;

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}}
,

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\frac{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}[\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}{\mathbf{S}}^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}})]}{E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{1-1/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}(X,{\mathbf{z}})\}}
.

4.3.3

MLE

The MLE is affected by assumptions of dependent covariate right-censoring/missingness because the specification of the likelihood changes in two main ways. The first is that the estimating equations now depend on
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
when assuming dependent right-censoring, and on
f_{X|R,{\mathbf{Z}}}
when assuming dependent missingness. Estimating these two distributions requires modeling the dependency between variables that are not simultaneously observed. That estimation is difficult since the two distributions may not be identifiable, unless certain assumptions are made (see Section
5
).

The second is that the domains of the integral with respect to
X
vary between the right-censored covariate problem and the missing covariate problem. The domain for the right-censored covariate problem is the same as when we assumed independent covariate right-censoring: the domain is all
X
greater than
C
. In the missing covariate problem, the domain of integration, denoted by
{\cal D}(X)
, varies. With dependent missingness, there may be times where the dependency between
X
and
R
informs a specific region
{\cal D}(X)
, such as all
X
higher or lower than a certain cutoff. However, more often than not, the integration domain
{\cal D}(X)
is not known. For example,
X
can have a non-monotone missingness pattern that makes it difficult to discern the exact domain
{\cal D}(X)

(
Little, 1992a
)
. In these settings, it may be impossible to compute the MLE , as its computation depends on unverifiable information.

4.3.4

Augmented estimators

For the ACC estimator, we still use the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}})
in the right-censored covariate problem, but now use
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(y,{\mathbf{z}})
for the missing covariate problem, which does not simplify to
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})
. For the MACC and AIPW estimators, we now use
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
and
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
. After applying these changes, the estimating equations for the ACC, MACC, and AIPW estimators under dependent right-censoring and missingness leads to identical estimating equations by interchanging
(W,\Delta)
with
(XR,R)
(Table
3
).
Therefore, applying the augmented estimators for the dependent missing covariate problem directly to the right-censored covariate problem lead to consistent results. Still, the partial information provided by the right-censored covariate requires fewer unverifiable assumptions.

In Sections
S.2.5
and
S.3.5
, we derive the augmented components the augmented estimators under dependent right-censoring, which match the representations found in
Bartlett et al. (2014a)
for the ACC estimator and
Rotnitzky & Robins (1997a)
for the AIPW estimator. To our knowledge, the MACC had not been previously proposed, making it a new addition to the estimators handling covariates under dependent right-censoring and missingness.

4.3.5

Guaranteeing statistical efficiency and reducing computation cost

Just as with independent covariate right-censoring and missingness, we can guarantee higher efficiency of the augmented estimators by updating the augmented component using a fixed matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
(Table
4
). We propose using
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm close}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
to define
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm close}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
across all augmented estimators. This choice grants us a closed form solution that reduces the computational burden associated with the estimation process and ensures the augmented estimators remain consistent—a result we prove in Theorem
1
.

Table 4
:
Changes to estimating equations for augmented estimators that guarantee increased efficiency used in the right-censored covariate problem when right-censoring is informative (i.e., dependent censoring) and in the missing covariate problem when missingness is not at random (i.e., dependent missingness).

Updating the augmented components
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
of Table
3

{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
, where
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
is as follows:

Augmented complete case (ACC) estimator

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}=-E[\{\Delta-{\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]E([\{\Delta-{\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm ACC,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}=-E[\{R-\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]E([\{R-\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

Modified augmented complete case (MACC) estimator

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}=-E[\{1-\Delta/{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(W,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm cens,ind}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]E([\{1-\Delta/{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(W,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm MACC,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}=-E[\{1-R/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm CC}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]E([\{1-R/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

Augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) estimator

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm cens,dep}=-E[\{1-\Delta/{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(W,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm cens,dep}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm cens};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]E([\{1-\Delta/{\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens,dep}(W,{\mathbf{Z}})}\}{{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\rm AIPW,eff}^{\rm miss,dep}=-E[\{1-R/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\rm IPW}^{\rm miss,dep}({\mathbf{O}}^{\rm miss};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}){\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]E([\{1-R/\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,dep}(X,{\mathbf{Z}})\}{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}]^{\otimes 2})^{-1}

Note: While the form of
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
can be that of Table
3
, any function of the same length as
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and only a function of
(Y,{\mathbf{Z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
can be used to define
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. Definition of notation and terms are as in Table
3
. Aside from minor notation differences, there are no major differences between the right-censored and missing covariate problems.

5

Implementing the estimators

Implementing the IPW, MLE, ACC, MACC, and AIPW estimators require specification of various distributions prior to estimating
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
:
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the right-censored covariate problem and
f_{X,R|{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
f_{R|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the missing covariate problem, respectively. For pedagogical purposes, we assume that the form of these distributions (e.g.,
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}\sim
bivariate normal) are known but the set of finite-dimensional parameters indexing these distributions are unknown. We refer to the parameters of these distributions as
nuisance parameters
since they are not of main interest, but knowing them is needed to estimate
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. There are two approaches to obtaining these nuisance parameters: a mechanistic approach in which the analyst
specifies
them, and an empirical approach in which the analyst
estimates
them.

5.1

Mechanistic approach to nuisance parameters

A mechanistic approach fully specifies the finite-dimensional parameters of the nuisance distributions (i.e., the nuisance parameters). For example, if
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(similar approach with
f_{X,R|{\mathbf{Z}}}
) follows a bivariate normal distribution, the mean and covariance parameters are explicitly specified. If
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
(and
f_{R|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) follows a logistic regression model, its parameters are specified. A mechanistic approach is convenient. It reduces the work needed to implement the estimators and simplifies the asymptotic variance for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, as it avoids the additional variability introduced by estimating the nuisance parameters. Still, correctly specifying a nuisance distribution and its parameters is not easy; even after decades of research to find and validate a model, the model may not be completely accurate. In Huntington disease, extensive research has focused on developing alternative measures to time to diagnosis for use in risk-group classifications. Yet,
Zhang et al. (2011a)
cautioned researchers against over-interpreting these alternative measures and using them as substitute measurements for the true time to diagnosis.

5.2

Empirical approach to nuisance paramaters

Let
\alpha
index the distribution
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Ensuring
\alpha
is identifiable requires, for example, that the covariance of
(X,C)
given
{\mathbf{Z}}
is known and
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
is sufficiently smooth
(
Ebrahimi et al., 2003a
)
. This covariance is zero for the case of independent covariate right-censoring, but not zero under covariate dependent right-censoring. When
\alpha
is identifiable, an empirical approach to estimating
\alpha
is to maximize the log-likelihood of
(W,\Delta)
given
{\mathbf{Z}}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}=\underset{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{\mathrm{argmax}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\delta_{i}\hbox{log}\int_{w_{i}}^{\infty}f_{C,X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,x,{\mathbf{z}};\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})dc+(1-\delta_{i})\hbox{log}\int_{w_{i}}^{\infty}f_{C,X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,x,{\mathbf{z}};\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})dx\right\}.

The resulting
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}
is a
\sqrt{n}
-consistent estimator (Section
S.2
).

A typical model for
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\delta=1,y,{\mathbf{z}})\equiv\hbox{pr}(\Delta=1|y,{\mathbf{z}})
(or
\hbox{pr}(R=1|y,{\mathbf{z}})
) is a generalized linear model with
\Delta
(or
R
) as the outcome and
(y,{\mathbf{z}})
as covariates, such as a logistic regression model with mean
\mu(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
. An empirical approach to estimating
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is using any software that implements logistic regression.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
index the distribution
f_{XR,R|{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Under dependent missingness,
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is non-identifiable based solely on data
(XR,R,{\mathbf{Z}})
; additional assumptions are needed. For example,
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is identifiable if the conditional covariance of
X
and
R
given
{\mathbf{Z}}
is known, and the domain of integration
{\cal D}(x)
is known. These are quite strong assumptions, however. When
X
is missing, positing a correct form of the conditional covariance of
X
and
R
given
{\mathbf{Z}}
is nearly impossible. Also, knowing the correct domain of integration
{\cal D}(x)
(i.e., the possible missing domain) is difficult when there is no particular pattern to the missingness.

With empirical approaches, the asymptotic variance of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
must account for the added variability from estimating nuisance parameters. Our proof of Theorem
1
(Sections
S.2
and
S.3
) shows the necessary adjustments to include this uncertainty.

6

Robustness, efficiency, and asymptotic properties of the estimators

All estimators are consistent and asymptotically normal in both the right-censored and missing covariate problems (Theorem
1
). The consistency and asymptotic normality hold even when some of the nuisance distributions are misspecified—a result that means the estimators are robust to misspecification. This robustness is crucial to correctly estimating the progression patterns of Huntington disease impairments. Proofs of the consistency, asymptotic normality, and robustness to misspecification are in Sections
S.2
and
S.3
.

Theorem
1

Assume
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}
for the right-censored and missing covariate problems. Let
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
be the solution to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}({\mathbf{O}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})={\mathbf{0}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}(\cdot)
is an estimating function corresponding to one of the six estimators evaluated at observed data
{\mathbf{O}}_{i}
. Under regularity conditions stated in
S.2.2
, the estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is consistent and asymptotically normal:

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})\rightarrow\hbox{Normal}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}{\mathbf{B}}{\mathbf{A}}^{\rm-T}),

where
{\mathbf{A}}=E\{\partial{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}({\mathbf{O}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})/\partial{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{{}^{\rm T}}\}
and
{\mathbf{B}}=E\{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}({\mathbf{O}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\}
. The influence function corresponding to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is
{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}=-{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}({\mathbf{O}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})
. The specific forms of
{\mathbf{A}}
,
{\mathbf{B}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}
are listed in
Sections
S.2
and
S.3
; forms are given for each estimator, when either a mechanistic or an empirical approach is used to specify or model the nuisance distributions. The estimators are shown to yield consistency and asymptotic normality under the misspecifications listed in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
Can the nuisance distribution be misspecified?

Note: “
” indicates that the density can misspecified, whereas
\times
signifies that the density needs to be correctly specified. Under dependent right-censoring and missingness, two options of decomposing
f_{C,X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
f_{R,X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
are provided.

Having established when consistency and asymptotically normality holds, an important next step is determining how the estimators compare in terms of statistical efficiency.

Theorem
2

Assume correct specification of distributions required for consistency and asymptotic normality listed in Theorem
1
. The statistical efficiency of the estimators, from most to least efficient, is

\displaystyle\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{MLE}})<\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{ACC}})\\
\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{MACC}})\end{array}<\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{CC}})\leq\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{AIPW}})<\hbox{var}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{\text{IPW}}).

The order of statistical efficiency is the same for the right-censored covariate problem under dependent censoring and for the missing covariate problem.

The proof of Theorem
2
is in Section
S.4
and rests on computing the variances of the estimators and establishing the order of increasing variance. Results from Theorems
1
and
2
together highlight the benefits and trade-offs of each estimator.

Whether the covariate right-censoring and missingness are independent or dependent, the CC and IPW estimators exhibit the highest level of robustness, maintaining consistency regardless of how all distributions, aside from
f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, are specified. That robustness means we can misspecify the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
in the IPW estimator as any function of
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
and still achieve a consistent estimator. This ability to misspecify is especially helpful when the covariate right-censoring and missingness are dependent, as correctly specifying the dependency between
C
and
X
(or
R
and
X
) in
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is difficult.

A few caveats about the IPW estimator are worth noting. When
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
, we proved in Theorem
1
that even if we misspecify the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, the IPW estimator will remain consistent in both the right-censored and missing covariate covariate problems. The consistency of the IPW estimator, however, is up to a point. One may believe that because we are free to misspecify the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, we may also use the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens}
. Modeling the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens}
rather than
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is easier because we always observe the data
(Y,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}})
, whereas we do not always observe the data
(X,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}})
. However, we prove in Section
S.2.3
that, for the right-censored covariate problem, using
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm cens}
instead of
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
in the IPW estimator will yield an inconsistent estimator regardless of whether the covariate right-censoring is independent or dependent.

That bias persists in the missing covariate problem when the missingness is dependent, but not when the missingness is independent. When the missingness is independent,
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss}(y,{\mathbf{z}})
reduces to
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss,ind}({\mathbf{z}})
because
R\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
; with that simplification, the IPW estimator remains consistent (Section
S.3
). That same simplification does not occur when the missingness is dependent, and the resulting IPW estimator with probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\rm miss}
is inconsistent. These results further highlight that when adapting estimators from the missing to the right-censored covariate problem, some properties carry over and others do not.

Still, the high robustness makes the CC and IPW estimators a safe choice, yet both have the lowest efficiency among all estimators. In stark contrast, the MLE is much more efficient, but less robust. When the covariate right-censoring or missingness are independent, the distribution
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
must be correctly specified to ensure the MLE yields a consistent estimator. That requirement persists in the dependent setting, where now the correct specification of
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the right-censored covariate problem and of
f_{X|R,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the missing covariate problem is essential to ensure the MLE produces a consistent estimator. When these distributions are correct, the MLE achieves optimal efficiency (Theorem
2
). This optimal efficiency makes the MLE still sought after, even when correctly specifying
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
,
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
, and
f_{X|R,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is difficult.

While none of the augmented estimators is more efficient than the MLE, the ACC and MACC estimators are more efficient than the CC estimator, just as the AIPW is more efficient than the IPW estimator. The robustness of the augmented estimators differs depending on the assumptions made about covariate right-censoring and missingness. Typically, augmented estimators are doubly robust, meaning they produce consistent estimators even if the probabilities (i.e.,
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
or
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) or the augmented component (i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}})
) are misspecified, though not both. However, the ACC, MACC, and AIPW estimators are
only
doubly robust when
X
is missing and the covariate missingness is independent. Otherwise, these three estimators are singly robust, meaning they yield a consistent estimator only when the probabilities are correctly specified (i.e.,
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for ACC and
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for MACC and AIPW). The single robustness property results from the augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
not always having mean zero, so correctly specifying the probabilities is necessary for consistency. The double or single robustness of the estimators persists with or without using
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
in both independent and dependent covariate right-censoring and missingness (Figure
2
). This difference between when we have single and double robustness further underscores that augmented estimators designed for the missing covariate problem cannot be blindly applied to the right-censored covariate problem.

Figure 2
:
Comparing the robustness and efficiency of augmented estimators with and without
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
under misspecification.

Note: Independent covariate right-censoring and missingness are defined as
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}(C,R)|{\mathbf{Z}}
, and dependent covariate right-censoring and missingness are defined as
X\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}(C,R)|{\mathbf{Z}}
. The probabilities
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
are reduced to
\pi_{{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the missing covariate problem under independent covariate missingness. “Consistent” indicates that the estimator is consistent, “Efficient” indicates that it obtains higher efficiency when compared to the original estimator, and “Biased” indicates that it is inconsistent.

7

Simulation study

We conducted a simulation study to assess the robustness and efficiency of the estimators in the right-censored covariate problem, specifically examining the effects of correctly and incorrectly specifying
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
. We did not study the impact of correctly and incorrectly specifying
f_{X,R|Z}
in the missing covariate problem as those results are well documented; see, for example,

Little (1992a)
.

7.1

Data generation and metrics for comparison

In all
N=3,000
simulations, we generated data with a sample size of
n=1,000
and a censoring rate of
50\%
. Data were generated from the regression model in Equation (
1
), where
m(X,Z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\beta_{0}+\beta_{AX}(A-X)+\beta_{Z}Z
,
\epsilon\sim\hbox{Normal}(0,1)
, and
A\sim\hbox{Normal}(0,1)
. The true parameter values were
\beta_{0}=1
,
\beta_{AX}=3
, and
\beta_{Z}=2
. We used
(A-X)
as opposed to
X
alone to mimic the case when time to diagnosis is of interest; here,
A
is the current age and
X
is the age of diagnosis, so
A-X
is the time to diagnosis. We generated data
(X,C,Z)
from a trivariate normal distribution where the covariance of
(C,X)
conditional on
Z
was zero and not zero to guarantee independent and dependent right-censoring, respectively.

We applied all estimators, plus two others: the oracle estimator, which estimates
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
using the full but unobserved data, and the naive estimator, which uses all observations but incorrectly treats
W
as
X
. The oracle estimator serves as a gold standard for comparison, and the naive estimator illustrates the worst-case scenario. We handled the unknown parameters of
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
in two ways: a mechanistic approach in which the nuisance parameters are specified and an empirical approach in which the nuisance parameters are estimated using maximum likelihood. When the nuisance parameters were specified, all estimators were applied under independent and dependent right-censoring. When the nuisance parameters were estimated, all estimators were considered for the independent right-censoring case and only correctly specified scenarios were evaluated: the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(w,{\mathbf{z}})
was calculated using
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,{\mathbf{z}};\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
, and
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}};\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
was used for the augmented estimators and the MLE. For dependent right-censoring,
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
is not always identifiable; therefore only estimators that do not require this distribution were evaluated: oracle, naive, CC, and ACC with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. To showcase that the ACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
in the dependent missing covariate problem can be used for the right-censored covariate problem, we modified the simulation study from
Bartlett et al. (2014a)
by letting
C=X-beta\{\exp(Z),1\}
when
X
was not observed. We lay out more explicit details of our simulation study in Section
S.5.4
.

We evaluated all estimators by computing the mean estimate of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(i.e.,
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{i}
) and its percent bias (i.e.,
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})/{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}
); the empirical standard deviation of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
across all simulations; and the empirical mean of the estimated standard errors (i.e.,
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{\rm SE}_{i}
). Estimated standard errors were computed using the asymptotic variances derived in our theorems, with all expectations replaced by empirical averages. Lastly, we calculated the empirical coverage of the estimated 95% confidence intervals.

7.2

Simulation results

The majority of the bias was present in the intercept coefficient,
\beta_{0}
, when bias was expected, as illustrated in Figures
S.1
and
S.3
. Therefore, our discussion focuses on results for
\beta_{0}
under independent right-censoring (Table
5
: Known nuisance parameters). For more simulation results, refer to Tables
S.1
-
S.4
and Figures
S.1
-
S.4
.

All estimators behaved as according to the claims in Theorems
1
and
2
whether
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
was estimated or assumed known. The CC estimator was consistent both when the covariate right-censoring was both independent and dependent. That consistency is preferred over the naive estimator, which is highly biased because it erroneously assumes
W
is the true
X
value when it is not. As expected, however, the CC estimator was inefficient: by deleting data, it was up to 43% less efficient compared to the oracle estimator (i.e.,
{\rm(SD_{\rm oracle}-SD_{CC})/SD_{CC}}
). The IPW estimator remained as robust as the CC estimator, but incorrect specification of the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
led to 13% reduced efficiency (i.e.,
{\rm(SD_{\rm IPW,incorrect}-SD_{\rm IPW,correct})/SD_{\rm IPW,correct}}
). Furthermore, the choice of the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the IPW estimator led to bias.

The MLE was unbiased and was the most efficient estimator when the density
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for dependent right-censoring) was correctly specified, but when it was incorrectly specified, the MLE was biased, and its coverage level was far from the 95% nominal level. These results show that while the MLE may achieve the highest efficiency of all estimators, it is sensitive to misspecification and careful modeling of nuisance distributions is necessary. All augmented estimators were consistent when the probabilities were correctly specified, regardless of whether the augmented component
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
was correctly specified. Incorrect specification of the probabilities, however, led to bias even when the augmented component was correctly specified. These results corroborate our theoretical claims in Theorem
1
, showing that augmented estimators are singly robust in the right-censored covariate problem.

The augmented estimators without
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
were only more efficient when both the probability of
X
being observed and the augmented component were correctly specified. When the augmented component was incorrectly specified but the probability was correctly specified, the augmented estimators were less efficient. For example, compared to the CC estimator, the MACC estimator was about 21%
less
efficient when only the probability was correctly specified but 3%
more
efficient when both were correctly specified. The flip-flopping between efficiency loss and gains was corrected when using
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
; with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
, the MACC estimator was 1% more efficient than the CC estimator even when using the incorrect form of
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. Aside from the benefits of guaranteed efficiency gains,
the MACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
was also much more computationally efficient than without
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
, thanks to the use of the closed-form augmented component,
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. On an M1 MacBook Air with 16 GB RAM, the MACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
required 2 minutes per simulation to converge, while the MACC estimator without
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
required up to 2 hours.
Similar results were observed for the ACC and AIPW estimators. These results show that using
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
improves computational efficiency since, with it, we may use the closed-form
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm updated}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
.

Among the three augmented estimators, the ACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
was the most straightforward to implement. It resulted in nearly unbiased and efficient estimates for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, with coverage probabilities close to 95% for both independent and dependent covariate right-censoring. This strong performance makes the ACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
our preferred choice.

8

Application to Huntington disease

Huntington disease is an inherited neurodegenerative disease caused by a triplet repeat expansion of cytosine-adenine-guanine (CAG) in the HTT gene; any individual with
\geq 40
CAG repeats will develop the disease with 100% certainty
(
The Huntington’s Disease Collaborative ResearchΩGroup, 1993a
)
. An individual is clinically diagnosed (or “diagnosed,” for short) when their motor abnormalities are unequivocal signs of Huntington disease
(
Group, 1996a
)
. The hallmark impairment of the disease is motor dysfunction, but cognitive dysfunction often appears years earlier and is a major concern for individuals affected by the disease, as it can impact their ability to function, drive, work, and maintain a quality of life
(
McAllister et al., 2021a
)
. An ongoing research goal is to quantify the progression of cognitive dysfunction in the period leading up to a diagnosis, known as the prodromal stage, since with that knowledge, researchers can test in clinical trials whether an experimental therapy can slow or stop cognitive dysfunction before irreparable damage is done
(
Tabrizi et al., 2022c
)
.

Toward this goal, several observational studies have collected data on the extent and timing of cognitive dysfunction before a diagnosis. However, these studies face a significant limitation: some individuals leave a study early or the study concludes before all individuals meet the diagnosis criteria, so the time of diagnosis is right-censored since it will occur after (to the right of) the individual’s last study visit, we just do not know when. Modeling cognitive dysfunction before diagnosis is thus a right-censored covariate problem since time
to
diagnosis, defined as the difference between observation time and the right-censored time
of
diagnosis, is used to capture the period before diagnosis.

We addressed this right-censored covariate problem using data from Enroll-HD, a non-interventional multi-centered (North America, Latin America, Europe, Asia, Australia, and New Zealand) observational study conducted to identify features that indicate the earliest onset of Huntington disease symptoms
(
Landwehrmeyer et al., 2017a
)
. The study is ongoing, and new participants are being accepted, but we analyzed data only up to the fourth revision; this includes patients who were admitted between 2012 and 2018. Local ethics approvals were obtained from all study sites and written informed consent was obtained from all patients.

Individually, we analyzed scores from the Symbol Digit Modality Test (SDMT), Stroop Word Reading Test (SWRT), Stroop Color Naming Test (SCNT), and the Categorical Verbal Fluency Test (CFT)
(
Smith, 1973a
;
Stroop, 1935a
;
Benton et al., 1994a
)
. Together, these scores reflect the coordination of visual scanning, working memory, fine motor speed, and concentration where higher scores reflect better cognitive health. We modeled each of the scores as a function of time to diagnosis while adjusting for disease burden, education, and sex (male or female) to account for the lifetime pathology associated with Huntington disease. The disease burden is quantified by the CAG-Age-Product (CAP), which is calculated using the formula CAP = (Age at Study Entry)
\times
(CAG-30) / 6.49
(
Warner et al., 2022a
)
. Educational attainment was recorded using the International Standard Classification of Education (ISCED), and it was dichotomized as (i) General Educational Development (GED)/High School (HS) equivalency or higher educational attainment (ISCED level
\leq
3) and (ii) lower educational attainment (ISCED level 0-2).

8.1

Analytical sample selection and statistical analysis

We analyzed data only from individuals who were genetically confirmed to have the Huntington gene mutation and who were 100% guaranteed to be diagnosed with the disease (i.e.,
\geq
40 CAG repeats). We removed individuals who, at study entry, had already met the criteria for a diagnosis, as we focused on the prodromal stage and those who had missing values for the outcome test scores (SDMT, SWRT, SCNT, CFT), sex, CAP, and/or education at baseline (
n=129,2.5\%
). The analytical sample further excluded patients from Latin America due to the small sample size from this group (
n=36,1\%
). Ultimately, our analysis was based on data from 4,943 individuals. Among them, 852 individuals had received a diagnosis, resulting in a censoring rate of time to diagnosis of 83%. The analytical sample was then analyzed cross-sectionally at baseline using the model:

\displaystyle{\rm Cognitive\ Score}_{i}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{\rm TTD}{\rm TTD}_{i}+\beta_{\rm CAPs}{\rm CAPs}_{i}+\beta_{\rm HS}{\rm I(HS)}_{i}+\beta_{\rm Female}{\rm I(Female)}_{i}+\epsilon_{i},

where for the
i^{th}
individual,
{\rm Cognitive\ Score}_{i}
represents one of the four cognitive test scores (i.e., scores on SDMT, SWRT, SCNT, or CFT);
{\rm TTD}_{i}
refers to time to diagnosis in years defined as age at baseline minus age at diagnosis;
{\rm CAPs}_{i}
is the CAP score;
{\rm I(HS)}_{i}
equals 1 if educational attainment is at least GED/High School and 0 if lower;
{\rm I(Female)}_{i}
is equal to 1 if the individual is female and 0 if male; and
\epsilon_{i}
is the random error. In this model,
{\rm TTD}_{i}
is the right-censored covariate, and having
{\rm TTD}_{i}=-1
indicates that the individual is one year away from their diagnosis.

We used the CC, IPW, MLE, ACC, MACC, and AIPW estimators to estimate the model parameters. All augmented estimators used
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. A bivariate normal distribution was used for
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
where the conditional covariance between
(X,C)
given
{\mathbf{Z}}
was zero to impose independent right-censoring. Distribution parameters
\alpha
for
f_{X,C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
were estimated using maximum likelihood, and these were used to define the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
and the distribution
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
for the MLE. The probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
used for the ACC estimator was estimated using logistic regression. The regression estimates of the CC and ACC estimators are valid for both independent and dependent right-censoring, whereas the IPW, AIPW, and MACC estimators assume independent right-censoring. The dependent right-censoring versions of the of IPW, MLE, MACC, and AIPW estimators were omitted from the analysis as they require stronger unverifiable assumptions than those made under independent right-censoring.

8.2

Results

Overall, the mean age at baseline was 40 years, and the majority of participants were female (59%), European (59%), Caucasian (94%), and had obtained a GED/High School diploma or higher educational level (89%). Similar to that of other studies, the conditional mean of age at diagnosis was 45 years
(
Tabrizi et al., 2022c
)
.

All estimators estimated a decrease in the cognitive test scores for each year closer to diagnosis (Table
6
)—a result that agrees with existing clinical studies that also showed cognitive dysfunction worsens as diagnosis approaches
(
Heim et al., 2020a
;
McAllister et al., 2021a
)
. However, the exact decrease—the effect estimate—differed across estimators, raising the question, which estimator(s) can we trust?

Our theoretical claims and simulation results lead us to trust the CC and ACC estimators most. The CC estimator is consistent regardless of whether the nuisance distributions are misspecified, and the ACC estimator is consistent as long as the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is correctly specified, which is possible since
(\Delta,Y,{\mathbf{Z}})
is fully observable. In fact, the ACC estimator produced effect estimates akin to those of the CC estimator, suggesting a correct specification of
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Between these two estimators, though both provide similarly reliable effect estimates, the higher efficiency of the ACC estimator makes it the preferred choice.

Compared to the CC and ACC estimators, the other estimators – IPW, AIPW, MACC, and MLE – yielded different effect estimates for time to diagnosis, all closer to the null. The differences in effect estimates likely result from the sensitivity of misspecifying nuisance distributions. For example, the expected decrease in SWRT score was
-3.06
(95% confidence interval:
-4.10,-2.02
) and
-2.48
(
-3.99,-0.97
) for the CC and IPW estimators, respectively. The CC and IPW estimators are equally robust to misspecification so long as the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
in the IPW estimator is a function of
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
. When
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is misspecified as a function of
(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})
, we proved the IPW estimator is no longer consistent (Section
S.2.3
). Our specification of
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
included education level, but education level can be considered a proxy measure for SWRT score (i.e., the outcome
Y
). Therefore, our
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
may actually be a function of
(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})
, not just
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
. This specification will bias the IPW estimator, which would explain why the effect estimates from the IPW estimator differed from those of the CC estimator.

Consistency of the AIPW and MACC estimators also depends on correct specification of
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Both will be inconsistent when
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is a function of
(Y,{\mathbf{Z}})
, following a similar argument as in Section
S.2.3
. Possible misspecification of
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
combined with the fact that multiple probabilities
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
were near
10^{-3}
at times may explain why the effect estimates from AIPW and MACC estimators differed from those of CC and ACC estimators. Finally, the different effect estimates from the MLE were likely due to the possible misspecification of
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
.

Our applied analysis is strong due to the large, well-characterized sample and the comparison of multiple estimators. However, there are some limitations. First, using the ISCED to define education level may not accurately equate years of education across different countries. Second, cognitive tests were conducted in the participants’ preferred languages, which could introduce variability. Third, our analysis is cross-sectional, based on baseline data, rather than longitudinal. As such, while our findings are consistent with previous studies that indicate cognitive decline as adults approach clinical diagnosis (e.g.,
Paulsen & Long, 2014a
), they are limited to associations and may not be interpreted as cognitive test score trajectories over time.

Discussion

We highlight in this paper the similarities and differences between six estimators—CC, IPW, MLE, ACC, MACC, and AIPW—applicable to the right-censored and missing covariate problems. All estimators except the MACC estimator have been proposed for the right-censored covariate problem, but only under independent covariate right-censoring. We establish that these estimators, including the MACC estimator, are not limited to just this setting and can be easily modified to handle dependent covariate right-censoring—a discovery that widens their use in the censored covariate literature.

The robustness and efficiency properties outlined in Theorems
1
and
2
can help analysts make an informed decision about which estimator to choose in practice. That decision is affected by what distributions the analyst is comfortable misspecifying and how much statistical efficiency is desired as estimators with higher statistical efficiency provide more precise statistical inference. Under the assumption that
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
, our recommendations for selecting an estimator, ordered from highest to lowest preference, are as follows:

1.

ACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
: Among all estimators, we recommend the ACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
as it is more efficient than the CC, AIPW, and IPW estimators. Although it requires correctly specifying the distribution
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
in the right-censored covariate problem, but this is actually feasible given that we
always
observe the data
(\Delta,Y,{\mathbf{Z}})
. Moreover, the ACC estimator is consistent under independent and dependent covariate right-censoring.

2.

MACC estimator with
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
: The MACC estimator also recovers some of the efficiency lost in the CC estimator, but its consistency hinges on the correct specification of
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(or
f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
for dependent covariate right-censoring). The parameters governing
f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
are not identifiable without unverifiable assumptions; therefore, ensuring
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(or
f_{C|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) is correctly specified is difficult.

3.

CC estimator: While deleting data is usually a statistical faux pas, the CC estimator remains consistent in both right-censored and missing covariate problems, regardless of whether the covariate right-censoring or missingness is independent or dependent. Therefore, we recommend the CC estimator when asymptotic unbiasedness is the main concern, but not when statistical efficiency is the primary interest.

4.

MLE: The MLE is less robust than the CC and IPW estimators when the covariate right-censoring and missingness is assumed independent or dependent. However, when there is confidence in correctly specifying
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(or
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
), the MLE is the preferred choice, as it will be a consistent estimator with optimal statistical efficiency.

5.

IPW and AIPW estimators: We discourage using the IPW and AIPW estimators, as both are less efficient than the CC estimator, which is simpler to implement. Moreover, the AIPW estimator is prone to bias when
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
is misspecified. Accurately specifying
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
in the dependent covariate right-censoring and missingness is not straightforward as the parameters governing this probability are not identifiable without additional, often unverifiable assumptions.

No matter which estimator is chosen, we recommend also using the CC estimator, as it is the most robust estimator so long as
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
. Under this assumption, any differences in effect estimates between the CC estimator and another estimator would indicate that the other estimator is inconsistent. A search can then take place to pinpoint the source of the inconsistency, such as misspecified nuisance distributions.

While the IPW estimator is the least of our preferred choices, it has been shown to reduce bias and/or improve efficiency over the CC estimator under
different
assumptions than we consider.
When
(\Delta,R)\mathrel{\not\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
—an assumption implied by
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})

(
Ashner & Garcia, 2024a
)
—the CC and IPW estimators are inconsistent for both right-censored and missing covariate problems. However, for the right-censored covariate problem,
Matsouaka & Atem (2020a)
showed that including
Y
in the probability for the IPW estimator corrects the bias present in the CC estimator. For the missing covariate problem,
Little et al. (2022a)
also describes how including
Y
in the probability for the IPW estimator corrects the bias present in the CC estimator and increases efficiency. However, this choice would lead to the IPW estimator producing bias when in fact
(C,R)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y|(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
.

Using our preferred ACC estimator in future Huntington disease data analyses could benefit ongoing research. Preventative clinical trials, which aim to slow or stop the disease before irreversible damage occurs, depend on understanding impairment progression patterns before diagnosis. Yet most analyses currently use either a CC estimator
(
Long et al., 2014a
)
, which sacrifices efficiency, or an imputation estimator, which conveniently replaces time to diagnosis with an estimated value
(
Scahill, 2020a
)
, but whose consistency requires correct specification of
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
(or
f_{X|C,{\mathbf{Z}}}
) when covariate right-censoring is independent (or dependent)
(
Bernhardt et al., 2015a
)
. The ACC estimator overcomes these limitations and can be used without testing whether the covariate right-censoring is independent or dependent, as its form remains the same in both scenarios. Not needing that type of test is advantageous in practice because when individuals drop out of Huntington observational studies, researchers often do not know why, except in rare instances. Without knowing why individuals drop out, it is difficult to know whether we should use estimators that assume independent or dependent covariate right-censoring. With the ACC estimator, we bypass this guessing game.

Our analysis of Enroll-HD data using the ACC estimator confirmed earlier findings that cognitive dysfunction worsens before diagnosis, but we can have more confidence in this conclusion because we now have more efficient estimates. These results, along with the proven properties of the ACC estimator, give us confidence to use it in future analyses where the progression patterns of impairment before diagnosis are unknown—insights that could help pinpoint when and how to best intervene in Huntington disease.

By thoroughly exploring right-censored and missing covariate problems, we clarified the necessary modifications for applying estimators to both. This exploration offered an unprecedented comparison of the estimators’ robustness to misspecification and statistical efficiency. While we assumed
f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}
followed a normal distribution, other distributions could be used, and even greater flexibility could be achieved by assuming a restricted mean model (i.e.,
E(\epsilon|X,{\mathbf{Z}})=0
). Our work thus equips analysts to understand the nuances and similarities of these problems, preventing blind decisions and helping them choose the correct estimator in practice.

Supplementary Material for
Establishing the Parallels and Differences Between Right-Censored and Missing Covariates

Jesus E. Vazquez, Marissa C. Ashner, Yanyuan Ma, Karen Marder, and Tanya P. Garcia

Throughout the Supplementary Material, we denote different (conditional) densities
f
with subscripts to differentiate between densities. For example,
f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,w,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
denotes the conditional density function of
Y
given
(X,{\mathbf{Z}})
evaluated at
Y=y
,
X=w
, and
{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{z}}
. Similarly,
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\cdot)
will be used to denote the conditional expectation with respect to the density
f_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
. For a vector or matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
, we define
{\mathbf{M}}^{\otimes 2}\equiv{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm T}
. When unspecified,
E(\cdot)\equiv E_{Y,W,\Delta,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\cdot)
for the right-censored covariate problem and
E(\cdot)\equiv E_{Y,XR,R,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\cdot)
for the missing covariate problem.

S.1

Closed form solution of
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm closed}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})

The augmentation term
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm closed}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
was used in the augmented estimators to increase computational speed in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(Tables
and
4
). That augmentation term can be written as

\displaystyle E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,y,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\frac{f_{X,Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,y,{\mathbf{z}})}{f_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,{\mathbf{z}})}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X,Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,y,{\mathbf{z}})dx}{\int f_{Y,X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}})dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})f_{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{z}})dx}{\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})f_{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{z}})dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx}{\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx}.

In the following subsections, we derive the closed-form expression. We consider the special case when
m\{X=x,{\mathbf{Z}}=(a,z);{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}(a-x)+\beta_{2}z
and make the following assumptions:

1.

X
is normally distributed with mean equal to
\mu_{x}
and variance equal to
\sigma^{2}_{x}
,

2.

\epsilon
is normally distributed with mean equal to zero and variance equal to
\sigma^{2}
.

S.1.1

Denominator

The denominator is

\displaystyle{\cal D}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{[y-\{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}(a-x)+\beta_{2}z\}]^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\exp\left\{-\frac{(x-\mu_{x})^{2}}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right\}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\int\exp\left[-\frac{\{(y-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}a-\beta_{2}z)+\beta_{1}x\}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]\exp\left\{-\frac{(x-\mu_{x})^{2}}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right\}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}\beta_{1}^{2}+2x\beta_{1}e_{*}+e^{*2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}-2x\mu_{x}+\mu_{x}^{2}}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\int\exp\biggr\{x^{2}\left(-\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right)+x\left(-\frac{\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\mu_{x}}{\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right)+\left(-\frac{e_{*}{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\mu_{x}^{2}}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right)\biggr\}dx,

where
e_{*}=y-(\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}a+\beta_{2}z)
and
\epsilon=\beta_{1}x+e_{*}
. The error
\epsilon
represents the model error from Equation (
1
) for the special case considered in this section.
At this step, let

\displaystyle a_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right);\quad b_{*}=\left(-\frac{\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\mu_{x}}{\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right);\quad c_{*}=\left(-\frac{e^{*2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\mu_{x}^{2}}{2\sigma_{x}^{2}}\right).

Then, continuing with our calculation of the denominator
{\cal D}
, we can complete the square to obtain

\displaystyle{\cal D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\int\exp(x^{2}a_{*}+xb_{*}+c_{*})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\int\exp\left\{a_{*}\left(x+b_{*}/2a_{*}\right)^{2}+c_{*}-b_{*}^{2}/4a_{*}\right\}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\exp\left(c_{*}-b_{*}^{2}/4a_{*}\right)\int\exp\{-(x-\mu_{*})^{2}/2\sigma_{*}^{2}\}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{*}^{2}}}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\exp\left(c_{*}-b_{*}^{2}/4a_{*}\right)\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{*}^{2}}}\exp\{-(x-\mu_{*})^{2}/2\sigma_{*}^{2}\}dx,

where
\mu_{*}=-b_{*}/(2a_{*})
and
\sigma_{*}^{2}=\sigma_{x}^{2}\sigma^{2}/(\sigma^{2}+\beta_{1}^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2})
.
Therefore, the denominator is

\displaystyle{\cal D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(\sigma^{2}+\beta_{1}^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2})}}\exp\left(c_{*}-\frac{b_{*}^{2}}{4a_{*}}\right).

S.1.2

Numerator

The numerator is a vector, with each element corresponding to the integral of the partial derivative of the log-likelihood with respect to
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\sigma\}
. The components of
{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{T}=\{\dot{\beta_{0}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}}),\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}}),\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}}),\dot{\sigma}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}^{T}
are equal to

\displaystyle\dot{\beta_{0}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\epsilon/\sigma^{2};\quad\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=(a-x)\epsilon/\sigma^{2};

\displaystyle\dot{\beta_{2}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=z\epsilon/\sigma^{2};\quad\dot{\sigma}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=-1/\sigma+\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{3}.

For
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{0}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
, the numerator takes the form

\displaystyle\int\dot{\beta_{0}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\int\frac{(\beta_{1}x+e_{*})}{\sigma^{2}}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left\{\beta_{1}\int xf_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx+e_{*}\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left\{\beta_{1}\int xf_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx+e_{*}{\cal D}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\beta_{1}{\cal D}\mu_{*}+e_{*}{\cal D}\right).

The last line follows since

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\int xf_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{*}^{2}}}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\exp\left(c_{*}-\frac{b_{*}^{2}}{4a_{*}}\right)\int x\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{*}^{2}}}\exp\biggr\{\frac{\left(x-\mu_{*}\right)^{2}}{-2\sigma_{*}^{2}}\biggr\}dx,

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{*}^{2}}}{2\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}}}\exp\left(c_{*}-\frac{b_{*}^{2}}{4a_{*}}\right)\mu_{*}=\beta_{1}{\cal D}\mu_{*}.

Since
z
is a constant, then
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{2}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}=(z/\sigma^{2})\left(\beta_{1}{\cal D}\mu_{*}+e_{*}{\cal D}\right)
.
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
takes the following form

\displaystyle\int\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\left\{\frac{(a-x)(\beta_{1}x+e_{*})}{\sigma^{2}}\right\}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\left\{-\beta_{1}x^{2}+x(a\beta_{1}-e_{*})+ae_{*}\right\}dx

since
(a-x)(\beta_{1}x+e_{*})=a\beta_{1}x+ae_{*}-\beta_{1}x^{2}-xe_{*}=-\beta_{1}x^{2}+x(a\beta_{1}-e_{*})+ae_{*}
. This simplification leads to

\displaystyle\int\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\biggr\{-\beta_{1}\int x^{2}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx+(a\beta_{1}-e_{*})\int xf_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad+ae_{*}\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\biggr\{-\beta_{1}\int x^{2}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx+(a\beta_{1}-e_{*}){\cal D}\mu_{*}+ae_{*}{\cal D}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left\{-\beta_{1}{\cal D}(\sigma_{*}^{2}+\mu_{*}^{2})+(a\beta_{1}-e_{*}){\cal D}\mu_{*}+ae_{*}{\cal D}\right\}.

The last line follows since

\displaystyle\int x^{2}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx={\cal D}E(X^{2})={\cal D}(\sigma_{*}^{2}+\mu_{*}^{2}).

Finally,
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\sigma}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
equals

\displaystyle\int\dot{\sigma}l(y,x,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx

\displaystyle=\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma}+\frac{(x\beta_{1}+e_{*})^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\right\}dx

\displaystyle=\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})\left\{x^{2}\left(\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)+x\left(\frac{2\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)+\left(\frac{{e_{*}}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)\right\}dx

\displaystyle=\biggr\{\left(\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)\int x^{2}f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx\biggr\}+\biggr\{\left(\frac{2\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)\int xf_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx\biggr\}

\displaystyle+\biggr\{\left(\frac{{e_{*}}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)\int f_{Y|X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})f_{x|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})dx\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\right){\cal D}\left(\sigma_{*}^{2}+\mu_{*}^{2}\right)+\left(\frac{2\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{3}}\right){\cal D}\mu_{*}+\left(\frac{{e_{*}}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma}\right){\cal D}.

S.1.3

Final expression of
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,X,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

With the closed forms of the numerator and denominator, the components of
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{{\mathbf{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{F}(y,x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}
take the form

\displaystyle E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{0}}l(y,X,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\beta_{1}\mu_{*}+e_{*}\right),

\displaystyle E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{1}}l(y,X,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left\{-\beta_{1}(\sigma_{*}^{2}+\mu_{*}^{2})+(a\beta_{1}-e_{*})\mu_{*}+ae_{*}\right\},

\displaystyle E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{2}}l(y,X,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{z}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\beta_{1}\mu_{*}+e_{*}\right),

\displaystyle E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\sigma}l(y,X,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)\left[\sigma_{*}^{2}+\mu_{*}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{2\beta_{1}e_{*}}{\sigma^{3}}\right)\mu_{*}+\left(\frac{{e_{*}}^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma}\right).

The remaining step multiplies each of the above components by
-1
to obtain
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{\rm closed}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. For an additional covariate
z_{p}
, the closed form solution is
E_{X|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}\{\dot{\beta_{p}}l(y,X,z;{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\}=z_{p}\left(\beta_{1}\mu_{*}+e_{*}\right)/\sigma^{2}
.

S.2

Proof of Theorem
1
for the right-censored covariate problem

We first prove Theorem
1
for the right-censored covariate problem. The proof is divided into multiple parts. First, we address the specification
or
estimation of nuisance distributions used in the estimators. Then, we prove consistency, and asymptotic normality. We first present results under independent covariate right-censoring, and then explain modifications made to adjust for dependent covariate right-censoring. To minimize notation, we drop the superscripts (e.g., “cens, ind”).

S.2.1

Specification or estimation of nuisance distributions
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
,
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}

Under independent covariate right-censoring, estimation of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
with the estimators in Table
1
requires specifying or estimating nuisance distributions—
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
,
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
, and/or
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
—as discussed in Section
5
. For example,

•

IPW estimator: Requires the density
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
to compute the probability

\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
;

•

MLE: Requires the density
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
;

•

ACC estimator: Requires the density
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}
to compute the probability
\pi_{Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(y,{\mathbf{z}})
and the density of
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
to compute
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
;

•

MACC and AIPW estimators: Requires the density
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}
to compute the probability
\pi_{X,{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}})
and the density
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}
to compute
{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
.

Throughout, we consider parametric models
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
,
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
, and
f_{\Delta|Y,{\mathbf{Z}}}(\delta,y,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})
, where
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})
is a set of finite-dimensional parameters; we refer to them as nuisance parameters. Under a
mechanistic
approach, the nuisance parameters are assumed known and are specified by the analyst. Under an
empirical
approach, the nuisance parameters need to be estimated. We show how these parameters are estimated and present the corresponding asymptotic properties.
We describe a logistic regression model for estimating
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
in Section
5
, therefore, we focus on the parameter set
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=({\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{T},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{T})^{T}
.

Estimating the parameters governing
f_{X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(x,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
and
f_{C|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,{\mathbf{z}};{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
separately is not possible, as they are not identifiable
(
Ebrahimi et al., 2003a
)
. Yet, under the assumption of independent covariate right-censoring, the parameters are identifiable when
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=({\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{T},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{T})^{T}
is jointly estimated using the density of
(W,\Delta)
conditional on
{\mathbf{Z}}
. The log-likelihood for one individual is given by,

\displaystyle\hbox{log}f_{W,\Delta|{\mathbf{Z}}}(w_{i},\delta_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})

\displaystyle=\delta_{i}\hbox{log}\biggr\{\int_{w_{i}\leq C}f_{C,X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(c,w_{i},{\mathbf{z}}_{i};\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})dc\biggr\}+(1-\delta_{i})\hbox{log}\biggr\{\int_{w_{i}\leq X}f_{C,X|{\mathbf{Z}}}(w_{i},x,{\mathbf{z}}_{i}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})dx\biggr\}
